People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… beyond work, I mean, I try to keep things together, so I don’t want to overstate it. But it has affected daily life in the sense that the guys at the firehouse are starting to… I don’t know, trust me less with details.

I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was pretty embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance to move into more of a leadership role is kind of slipping away because the crew can’t always count on me to track everything consistently.

I know that still sounds work-related, but it’s also relationships, you know? Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?

I’ve tried to just push through it, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately it’s started to get me in some trouble at the firehouse, so… I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess so. I don’t think of it as a big dramatic thing, but if I’m sitting in a meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been messing with whatever’s on the table in front of me without even noticing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to be careful, especially with paperwork, but that does happen more than I’d like. Like I’ll finish an incident report and think it’s solid, and then the captain will point out I put the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or skipped one of those little boxes that ends up mattering for the safety review.  \n\nIt’s not like I don’t care—I do—but I guess I miss details I should probably be catching. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but at the station, if the guys are talking through a plan and I feel like I already see the answer, I’ll jump in before they’re done.\n\nUsually I realize a second later that I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. I’m not trying to take over, it’s just… it comes out before I’ve really thought about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being little, but my mom says I was kind of like this back in grade school too.\n\nShe’ll say I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… and I’d get notes sent home. Not because I couldn’t do the work, exactly—she always says I was bright enough—but I guess I had trouble keeping myself on track. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention in multiple areas of your life, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does show up in more than one place. I try not to make excuses for it, but at work, like during briefings or drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the map or the screen and I didn’t catch half of what command just said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know that stuff matters. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out. But yeah, I guess the focus part follows me around more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily irritated?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated like anybody, especially if things feel chaotic or I’m being pulled in a bunch of directions, but… I don’t know that it’s this big up-and-down thing.\n\nMy wife might have a better read on that than me, honestly. I try not to take it out on people. If you mean do I snap sometimes or get short, maybe once in a while, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life beyond just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… beyond work, I mean, I try to keep things together, so I don’t want to overstate it. But it has affected daily life in the sense that the guys at the firehouse are starting to… I don’t know, trust me less with details.\n\nI got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was pretty embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance to move into more of a leadership role is kind of slipping away because the crew can’t always count on me to track everything consistently.\n\nI know that still sounds work-related, but it’s also relationships, you know? Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have an inability to engage in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can sit still if I have to, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything. But in command meetings especially, it feels like my body is just revving too high.\n\nLike I’m sitting there trying to look normal and pay attention, but inside I’m thinking I need to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—do something instead of just staying planted in the chair. Quiet downtime is hard for me that way sometimes. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I mean, not all the time, but it does happen.

If somebody gives me, say, three or four station tasks, I’ll usually jump right into the first one and I’m good at first. But then I’ll get pulled into something else, or notice something else that needs doing, and later I’ll realize the hose inventory or the equipment checklist is just sitting there half-done.

I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just blowing things off, because I’m really not trying to. It’s more like I lose the thread unless I’m really staying on myself. Does that answer what you mean?

Yeah, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just unreliable across the board, because I do care and I try to get things done.

Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s probably similar, just maybe less obvious because there aren’t formal checklists.

I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being little, but my mom says I was kind of like this back in grade school too.\n\nShe’ll say I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… and I’d get notes sent home. Not because I couldn’t do the work, exactly—she always says I was bright enough—but I guess I had trouble keeping myself on track. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention in multiple areas of your life, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does show up in more than one place. I try not to make excuses for it, but at work, like during briefings or drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the map or the screen and I didn’t catch half of what command just said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know that stuff matters. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out. But yeah, I guess the focus part follows me around more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily irritated?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated like anybody, especially if things feel chaotic or I’m being pulled in a bunch of directions, but… I don’t know that it’s this big up-and-down thing.\n\nMy wife might have a better read on that than me, honestly. I try not to take it out on people. If you mean do I snap sometimes or get short, maybe once in a while, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life beyond just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… beyond work, I mean, I try to keep things together, so I don’t want to overstate it. But it has affected daily life in the sense that the guys at the firehouse are starting to… I don’t know, trust me less with details.\n\nI got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was pretty embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance to move into more of a leadership role is kind of slipping away because the crew can’t always count on me to track everything consistently.\n\nI know that still sounds work-related, but it’s also relationships, you know? Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have an inability to engage in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can sit still if I have to, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything. But in command meetings especially, it feels like my body is just revving too high.\n\nLike I’m sitting there trying to look normal and pay attention, but inside I’m thinking I need to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—do something instead of just staying planted in the chair. Quiet downtime is hard for me that way sometimes. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever try any strategies to help you manage your behavior or focus when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has said I was kind of like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, losing homework… that kind of thing. I guess there were notes sent home too, even though I was bright enough to do the work. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a “bad kid” or anything, but yeah, apparently teachers were trying to manage me a bit. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse or saying something without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be rude or anything, but I probably do that.\n\nAt the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I already see what I think the answer is, I’ll cut in before somebody’s finished. And then a second later I realize I kind of stepped on them or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nIt’s not like I mean to dismiss people, but I can see how it comes across that way. That counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Did you have significant difficulty with organization or time management as a child?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clean memory of being specifically bad with time management as a kid.\n\nI know my mom has mentioned school stuff was a struggle sometimes, but organization specifically… I’d have to ask her, I think. I don’t want to overstate it if I’m not remembering it clearly. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home, similar to what you've described happening at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nIf somebody gives me, say, three or four station tasks, I’ll usually jump right into the first one and I’m good at first. But then I’ll get pulled into something else, or notice something else that needs doing, and later I’ll realize the hose inventory or the equipment checklist is just sitting there half-done.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just blowing things off, because I’m really not trying to. It’s more like I lose the thread unless I’m really staying on myself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in multiple areas of your life, such as at home and at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just unreliable across the board, because I do care and I try to get things done.\n\nBut at the firehouse, if someone gives me three or four station tasks, I’ll usually start strong on the first one. Then I get pulled into something else, or I notice something else that needs attention, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or an equipment checklist just sitting there half-finished.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not only one setting. It shows up in more than one area, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work where you have trouble paying attention or following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s probably similar, just maybe less obvious because there aren’t formal checklists.\n\nLike if someone asks me to do a few things, I’ll start one of them and have every intention of finishing all of it, but then I get pulled into something else and later realize I left part of it undone.\n\nAt the firehouse it’s clearer, like I said — if I’m given three or four station tasks, I’ll start strong on the first one, then end up sidetracked, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or equipment checklist sitting half-done. I’m not trying to ignore it or anything. It’s more like I lose track of what I was supposed to be finishing. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work where you have trouble paying attention or following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s probably similar, just maybe less obvious because there aren’t formal checklists.\n\nLike if someone asks me to do a few things, I’ll start one of them and have every intention of finishing all of it, but then I get pulled into something else and later realize I left part of it undone.\n\nAt the firehouse it’s clearer, like I said — if I’m given three or four station tasks, I’ll start strong on the first one, then end up sidetracked, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or equipment checklist sitting half-done. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDid your symptoms, such as difficulty paying attention or acting on impulse, start before the age of 12?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / firefighter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"My mom says I was like this back in grade school too—always out of my seat, blurting answers, losing homework, and getting notes sent home even though I was bright enough to do the work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has told me I was like this even back in grade school.

She says I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… that kind of thing. And I guess notes got sent home, even though I was bright enough to do the work.

I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but from what she says, yeah, it started pretty young — before 12. Does that answer it?

costing me trust at the firehouse; I was pulled into a performance and safety meeting, and now my shot at moving into leadership feels like it’s slipping because the crew can’t count on me to track the details consistently.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: It’s been a real problem for about a year and five months, ever since the longer rotating shifts and extra documentation and drone duties got added.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / firefighter / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in multiple areas of your life, such as at home and at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just unreliable across the board, because I do care and I try to get things done.\n\nBut at the firehouse, if someone gives me three or four station tasks, I’ll usually start strong on the first one. Then I get pulled into something else, or I notice something else that needs attention, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or an equipment checklist just sitting there half-finished.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not only one setting. It shows up in more than one area, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work where you have trouble paying attention or following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s probably similar, just maybe less obvious because there aren’t formal checklists.\n\nLike if someone asks me to do a few things, I’ll start one of them and have every intention of finishing all of it, but then I get pulled into something else and later realize I left part of it undone.\n\nAt the firehouse it’s clearer, like I said — if I’m given three or four station tasks, I’ll start strong on the first one, then end up sidetracked, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or equipment checklist sitting half-done. I’m not trying to ignore it or anything. It’s more like I lose track of what I was supposed to be finishing. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms, such as difficulty paying attention or acting on impulse, start before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has told me I was like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… that kind of thing. And I guess notes got sent home, even though I was bright enough to do the work.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but from what she says, yeah, it started pretty young — before 12. Does that answer it?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and following through on tasks, or with controlling your impulses and activity level, or is it kind of an equal mix of both?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I feel like I’m kind of always running hot, if that makes sense—like I can’t really shut the motor off. I’ve tried to just push through it, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately it’s started to get me in some trouble at the firehouse, so… I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess so. I don’t think of it as a big dramatic thing, but if I’m sitting in a meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been messing with whatever’s on the table in front of me without even noticing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to be careful, especially with paperwork, but that does happen more than I’d like. Like I’ll finish an incident report and think it’s solid, and then the captain will point out I put the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or skipped one of those little boxes that ends up mattering for the safety review.  \n\nIt’s not like I don’t care—I do—but I guess I miss details I should probably be catching. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but at the station, if the guys are talking through a plan and I feel like I already see the answer, I’ll jump in before they’re done.\n\nUsually I realize a second later that I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. I’m not trying to take over, it’s just… it comes out before I’ve really thought about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being little, but my mom says I was kind of like this back in grade school too.\n\nShe’ll say I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… and I’d get notes sent home. Not because I couldn’t do the work, exactly—she always says I was bright enough—but I guess I had trouble keeping myself on track. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention in multiple areas of your life, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does show up in more than one place. I try not to make excuses for it, but at work, like during briefings or drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the map or the screen and I didn’t catch half of what command just said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know that stuff matters. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out. But yeah, I guess the focus part follows me around more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily irritated?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated like anybody, especially if things feel chaotic or I’m being pulled in a bunch of directions, but… I don’t know that it’s this big up-and-down thing.\n\nMy wife might have a better read on that than me, honestly. I try not to take it out on people. If you mean do I snap sometimes or get short, maybe once in a while, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life beyond just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… beyond work, I mean, I try to keep things together, so I don’t want to overstate it. But it has affected daily life in the sense that the guys at the firehouse are starting to… I don’t know, trust me less with details.\n\nI got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was pretty embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance to move into more of a leadership role is kind of slipping away because the crew can’t always count on me to track everything consistently.\n\nI know that still sounds work-related, but it’s also relationships, you know? Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have an inability to engage in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can sit still if I have to, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything. But in command meetings especially, it feels like my body is just revving too high.\n\nLike I’m sitting there trying to look normal and pay attention, but inside I’m thinking I need to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—do something instead of just staying planted in the chair. Quiet downtime is hard for me that way sometimes. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever try any strategies to help you manage your behavior or focus when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has said I was kind of like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, losing homework… that kind of thing. I guess there were notes sent home too, even though I was bright enough to do the work. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a “bad kid” or anything, but yeah, apparently teachers were trying to manage me a bit. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse or saying something without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be rude or anything, but I probably do that.\n\nAt the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I already see what I think the answer is, I’ll cut in before somebody’s finished. And then a second later I realize I kind of stepped on them or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nIt’s not like I mean to dismiss people, but I can see how it comes across that way. That counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Did you have significant difficulty with organization or time management as a child?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clean memory of being specifically bad with time management as a kid.\n\nI know my mom has mentioned school stuff was a struggle sometimes, but organization specifically… I’d have to ask her, I think. I don’t want to overstate it if I’m not remembering it clearly. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home, similar to what you've described happening at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nIf somebody gives me, say, three or four station tasks, I’ll usually jump right into the first one and I’m good at first. But then I’ll get pulled into something else, or notice something else that needs doing, and later I’ll realize the hose inventory or the equipment checklist is just sitting there half-done.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just blowing things off, because I’m really not trying to. It’s more like I lose the thread unless I’m really staying on myself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in multiple areas of your life, such as at home and at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just unreliable across the board, because I do care and I try to get things done.\n\nBut at the firehouse, if someone gives me three or four station tasks, I’ll usually start strong on the first one. I’m not trying to ignore it or anything. It’s more like I lose track of what I was supposed to be finishing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and following through on tasks, or with controlling your impulses and activity level, or is it kind of an equal mix of both?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… kind of both, I guess. I guess I’m here because I feel like I’m kind of always running hot, if that makes sense—like I can’t really shut the motor off. I’ve tried to just push through it, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately it’s started to get me in some trouble at the firehouse, so… I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess so. I don’t think of it as a big dramatic thing, but if I’m sitting in a meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been messing with whatever’s on the table in front of me without even noticing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to be careful, especially with paperwork, but that does happen more than I’d like. Like I’ll finish an incident report and think it’s solid, and then the captain will point out I put the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or skipped one of those little boxes that ends up mattering for the safety review.  \n\nIt’s not like I don’t care—I do—but I guess I miss details I should probably be catching. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but at the station, if the guys are talking through a plan and I feel like I already see the answer, I’ll jump in before they’re done.\n\nUsually I realize a second later that I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. I’m not trying to take over, it’s just… it comes out before I’ve really thought about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being little, but my mom says I was kind of like this back in grade school too.\n\nShe’ll say I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… and I’d get notes sent home. Not because I couldn’t do the work, exactly—she always says I was bright enough—but I guess I had trouble keeping myself on track. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention in multiple areas of your life, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does show up in more than one place. I try not to make excuses for it, but at work, like during briefings or drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the map or the screen and I didn’t catch half of what command just said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know that stuff matters. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out. But yeah, I guess the focus part follows me around more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily irritated?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated like anybody, especially if things feel chaotic or I’m being pulled in a bunch of directions, but… I don’t know that it’s this big up-and-down thing.\n\nMy wife might have a better read on that than me, honestly. I try not to take it out on people. If you mean do I snap sometimes or get short, maybe once in a while, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life beyond just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… beyond work, I mean, I try to keep things together, so I don’t want to overstate it. Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have an inability to engage in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can sit still if I have to, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything. But in command meetings especially, it feels like my body is just revving too high.\n\nLike I’m sitting there trying to look normal and pay attention, but inside I’m thinking I need to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—do something instead of just staying planted in the chair. Quiet downtime is hard for me that way sometimes. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever try any strategies to help you manage your behavior or focus when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has said I was kind of like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, losing homework… that kind of thing. I guess there were notes sent home too, even though I was bright enough to do the work. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a “bad kid” or anything, but yeah, apparently teachers were trying to manage me a bit. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse or saying something without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be rude or anything, but I probably do that.\n\nAt the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I already see what I think the answer is, I’ll cut in before somebody’s finished. And then a second later I realize I kind of stepped on them or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nIt’s not like I mean to dismiss people, but I can see how it comes across that way. That counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Did you have significant difficulty with organization or time management as a child?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clean memory of being specifically bad with time management as a kid.\n\nI know my mom has mentioned school stuff was a struggle sometimes, but organization specifically… I’d have to ask her, I think. I don’t want to overstate it if I’m not remembering it clearly. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home, similar to what you've described happening at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nIf somebody gives me, say, three or four station tasks, I’ll usually jump right into the first one and I’m good at first. But then I’ll get pulled into something else, or notice something else that needs doing, and later I’ll realize the hose inventory or the equipment checklist is just sitting there half-done.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just blowing things off, because I’m really not trying to. It’s more like I lose the thread unless I’m really staying on myself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in multiple areas of your life, such as at home and at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. And now it feels like my chance to move into more of a leadership role is kind of slipping away because the crew can’t always count on me to track everything consistently.\n\nI know that still sounds work-related, but it’s also relationships, you know? Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have an inability to engage in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can sit still if I have to, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything. But in command meetings especially, it feels like my body is just revving too high.\n\nLike I’m sitting there trying to look normal and pay attention, but inside I’m thinking I need to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—do something instead of just staying planted in the chair. Quiet downtime is hard for me that way sometimes. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever try any strategies to help you manage your behavior or focus when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has said I was kind of like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, losing homework… that kind of thing. I guess there were notes sent home too, even though I was bright enough to do the work. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a “bad kid” or anything, but yeah, apparently teachers were trying to manage me a bit. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse or saying something without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be rude or anything, but I probably do that.\n\nAt the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I already see what I think the answer is, I’ll cut in before somebody’s finished. And then a second later I realize I kind of stepped on them or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nIt’s not like I mean to dismiss people, but I can see how it comes across that way. That counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Did you have significant difficulty with organization or time management as a child?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clean memory of being specifically bad with time management as a kid.\n\nI know my mom has mentioned school stuff was a struggle sometimes, but organization specifically… I’d have to ask her, I think. I don’t want to overstate it if I’m not remembering it clearly. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home, similar to what you've described happening at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nIf somebody gives me, say, three or four station tasks, I’ll usually jump right into the first one and I’m good at first. But then I’ll get pulled into something else, or notice something else that needs doing, and later I’ll realize the hose inventory or the equipment checklist is just sitting there half-done.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just blowing things off, because I’m really not trying to. It’s more like I lose the thread unless I’m really staying on myself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in multiple areas of your life, such as at home and at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just unreliable across the board, because I do care and I try to get things done.\n\nBut at the firehouse, if someone gives me three or four station tasks, I’ll usually start strong on the first one. Then I get pulled into something else, or I notice something else that needs attention, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or an equipment checklist just sitting there half-finished.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not only one setting. It shows up in more than one area, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work where you have trouble paying attention or following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s probably similar, just maybe less obvious because there aren’t formal checklists.\n\nLike if someone asks me to do a few things, I’ll start one of them and have every intention of finishing all of it, but then I get pulled into something else and later realize I left part of it undone.\n\nAt the firehouse it’s clearer, like I said — if I’m given three or four station tasks, I’ll start strong on the first one, then end up sidetracked, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or equipment checklist sitting half-done. I’m not trying to ignore it or anything. It’s more like I lose track of what I was supposed to be finishing. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms, such as difficulty paying attention or acting on impulse, start before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has told me I was like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… that kind of thing. And I guess notes got sent home, even though I was bright enough to do the work.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but from what she says, yeah, it started pretty young — before 12. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and following through on tasks, or with controlling your impulses and activity level, or is it kind of an equal mix of both?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… kind of both, I guess. I don’t want to overstate it, but the activity level side is a big part of why I’m here.\n\nIt feels like I’m always running hot, like I can’t really shut the motor off. And lately that’s been causing problems at the firehouse, so I figured I should actually talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nSo yeah, not just attention — the impulse and energy part is definitely there too. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are primarily with attention and disorganization, or is it more about impulsivity and hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the impulsivity and hyperactivity side, if I had to pick.\n\nI mean, the attention stuff is there too, but the reason I finally came in is because I feel like I’m always running hot — like I can’t shut the motor off. And lately that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that constant activity thing is probably the bigger problem right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life to the point where you feel like you're struggling to function normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m managing it okay, but honestly it has started to affect things pretty seriously.\n\nAt the firehouse, it’s costing me trust. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was… embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance of moving into a leadership role is kind of slipping away, because the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to keep track of the details consistently.\n\nI don’t want to make excuses or sound like I’m falling apart, but yeah — it’s impacting daily life and work enough that I’m struggling. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and following through on tasks, or with controlling your impulses and activity level, or is it kind of an equal mix of both?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… kind of both, I guess. I don’t want to overstate it, but the activity level side is a big part of why I’m here.\n\nIt feels like I’m always running hot, like I can’t really shut the motor off. And lately that’s been causing problems at the firehouse, so I figured I should actually talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nSo yeah, not just attention — the impulse and energy part is definitely there too. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are primarily with attention and disorganization, or is it more about impulsivity and hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the impulsivity and hyperactivity side, if I had to pick.\n\nI mean, the attention stuff is there too, but the reason I finally came in is because I feel like I’m always running hot — like I can’t shut the motor off. And lately that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that constant activity thing is probably the bigger problem right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life to the point where you feel like you're struggling to function normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m managing it okay, but honestly it has started to affect things pretty seriously.\n\nAt the firehouse, it’s costing me trust. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was… embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance of moving into a leadership role is kind of slipping away, because the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to keep track of the details consistently.\n\nI don’t want to make excuses or sound like I’m falling apart, but yeah — it’s impacting daily life and work enough that I’m struggling. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms caused significant problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / firefighter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"Even on days off, I have a hard time doing quiet stuff with my family because I start talking, moving around, checking tools in the garage, or getting up before I’ve been sitting long.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, not like huge fights or anything, but it does show up at home.

Even on my days off, when I’m supposed to just be with my family, I have a hard time doing the quiet stuff. Like I’ll sit down for a movie or something and before long I’m talking, shifting around, getting up, going out to the garage to check tools, or finding some little thing to do.

I don’t think I’m trying to avoid them or anything… I just don’t stay settled very well. And I can tell it probably gets old for them. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I feel like I’m kind of always running hot, if that makes sense—like I can’t really shut the motor off. I’ve tried to just push through it, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately it’s started to get me in some trouble at the firehouse, so… I figured I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess so. I don’t think of it as a big dramatic thing, but if I’m sitting in a meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’ll realize I’ve been messing with whatever’s on the table in front of me without even noticing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to be careful, especially with paperwork, but that does happen more than I’d like. Like I’ll finish an incident report and think it’s solid, and then the captain will point out I put the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or skipped one of those little boxes that ends up mattering for the safety review.  \n\nIt’s not like I don’t care—I do—but I guess I miss details I should probably be catching. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but at the station, if the guys are talking through a plan and I feel like I already see the answer, I’ll jump in before they’re done.\n\nUsually I realize a second later that I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. I’m not trying to take over, it’s just… it comes out before I’ve really thought about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being little, but my mom says I was kind of like this back in grade school too.\n\nShe’ll say I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… and I’d get notes sent home. Not because I couldn’t do the work, exactly—she always says I was bright enough—but I guess I had trouble keeping myself on track. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention in multiple areas of your life, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does show up in more than one place. I try not to make excuses for it, but at work, like during briefings or drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the map or the screen and I didn’t catch half of what command just said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know that stuff matters. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out. But yeah, I guess the focus part follows me around more than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily irritated?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them mood swings, exactly. I mean, I can get irritated like anybody, especially if things feel chaotic or I’m being pulled in a bunch of directions, but… I don’t know that it’s this big up-and-down thing.\n\nMy wife might have a better read on that than me, honestly. I try not to take it out on people. If you mean do I snap sometimes or get short, maybe once in a while, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life beyond just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… beyond work, I mean, I try to keep things together, so I don’t want to overstate it. But it has affected daily life in the sense that the guys at the firehouse are starting to… I don’t know, trust me less with details.\n\nI got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was pretty embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance to move into more of a leadership role is kind of slipping away because the crew can’t always count on me to track everything consistently.\n\nI know that still sounds work-related, but it’s also relationships, you know? Those are people I rely on, and I don’t want them thinking I’m careless. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have an inability to engage in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can sit still if I have to, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything. But in command meetings especially, it feels like my body is just revving too high.\n\nLike I’m sitting there trying to look normal and pay attention, but inside I’m thinking I need to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—do something instead of just staying planted in the chair. Quiet downtime is hard for me that way sometimes. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever try any strategies to help you manage your behavior or focus when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has said I was kind of like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, losing homework… that kind of thing. I guess there were notes sent home too, even though I was bright enough to do the work. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a “bad kid” or anything, but yeah, apparently teachers were trying to manage me a bit. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse or saying something without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be rude or anything, but I probably do that.\n\nAt the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I already see what I think the answer is, I’ll cut in before somebody’s finished. And then a second later I realize I kind of stepped on them or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nIt’s not like I mean to dismiss people, but I can see how it comes across that way. That counts, right?\n\nDoctor: Did you have significant difficulty with organization or time management as a child?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not totally sure. I don’t have a clean memory of being specifically bad with time management as a kid.\n\nI know my mom has mentioned school stuff was a struggle sometimes, but organization specifically… I’d have to ask her, I think. I don’t want to overstate it if I’m not remembering it clearly. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home, similar to what you've described happening at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nIf somebody gives me, say, three or four station tasks, I’ll usually jump right into the first one and I’m good at first. But then I’ll get pulled into something else, or notice something else that needs doing, and later I’ll realize the hose inventory or the equipment checklist is just sitting there half-done.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just blowing things off, because I’m really not trying to. It’s more like I lose the thread unless I’m really staying on myself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in multiple areas of your life, such as at home and at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just unreliable across the board, because I do care and I try to get things done.\n\nBut at the firehouse, if someone gives me three or four station tasks, I’ll usually start strong on the first one. Then I get pulled into something else, or I notice something else that needs attention, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or an equipment checklist just sitting there half-finished.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not only one setting. It shows up in more than one area, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work where you have trouble paying attention or following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it’s probably similar, just maybe less obvious because there aren’t formal checklists.\n\nLike if someone asks me to do a few things, I’ll start one of them and have every intention of finishing all of it, but then I get pulled into something else and later realize I left part of it undone.\n\nAt the firehouse it’s clearer, like I said — if I’m given three or four station tasks, I’ll start strong on the first one, then end up sidetracked, and later I’ll find the hose inventory or equipment checklist sitting half-done. I’m not trying to ignore it or anything. It’s more like I lose track of what I was supposed to be finishing. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms, such as difficulty paying attention or acting on impulse, start before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t remember every detail myself, but my mom has told me I was like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always getting out of my seat, blurting out answers, losing homework… that kind of thing. And I guess notes got sent home, even though I was bright enough to do the work.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but from what she says, yeah, it started pretty young — before 12. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and following through on tasks, or with controlling your impulses and activity level, or is it kind of an equal mix of both?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… kind of both, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and following through on tasks, or with controlling your impulses and activity level, or is it kind of an equal mix of both?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… kind of both, I guess. I don’t want to overstate it, but the activity level side is a big part of why I’m here.\n\nIt feels like I’m always running hot, like I can’t really shut the motor off. And lately that’s been causing problems at the firehouse, so I figured I should actually talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nSo yeah, not just attention — the impulse and energy part is definitely there too. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are primarily with attention and disorganization, or is it more about impulsivity and hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the impulsivity and hyperactivity side, if I had to pick.\n\nI mean, the attention stuff is there too, but the reason I finally came in is because I feel like I’m always running hot — like I can’t shut the motor off. And lately that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that constant activity thing is probably the bigger problem right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your relationships or daily life to the point where you feel like you're struggling to function normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m managing it okay, but honestly it has started to affect things pretty seriously.\n\nAt the firehouse, it’s costing me trust. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was… embarrassing. And now it feels like my chance of moving into a leadership role is kind of slipping away, because the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to keep track of the details consistently.\n\nI don’t want to make excuses or sound like I’m falling apart, but yeah — it’s impacting daily life and work enough that I’m struggling.
